D169 — Closed: the work order's heading claimed the band order over a list the exposure sort had replaced (Medium)

The same run's table led with HtmlParserTest and the prompt with the risk-pattern class, while the table's heading read "Ordered by what it costs to leave against what it costs to fix" and the prompt's "The first items are the highest value for the least change".

Both lists come from report["work_order"]. With an economic context configured, reorder_by_exposure (ADR 004) re-sorts it by recurrence and churn and ignores band; the prompt then promotes risk-5 classes and keeps one item per class. Neither heading described the order it sat over, so the reader saw two different first items and two claims that each was the priority. standard.md said class_delta "is what the ordering uses", which is false whenever the exposure sort ran.

Population. Both work-order skins — bounded and complete Markdown — and the remediation prompt's order sentence.

No ordering changed. The heading states the sort that produced the list, read from the same condition build_report applies it under (economic_impact is attached exactly when the exposure sort runs). The prompt's sentence describes its own rule rather than a value claim. standard.md states both orders.

It surfaced a question for decision rather than for this fix: ADR 007 §3 rule 5 says Fill-Ins never appear above Quick Wins, and the exposure sort could place them there. Decided and closed as D170.

Closing test: test_the_work_order_heading_states_the_order_the_list_is_in in tests/test_work_items_state_their_own_finding.py.

Mutation: only the complete Markdown report ignores the exposure sort — the demo showed the bounded chat view — and the test fails on its heading.

D170 — Closed: with an economic context configured, the work order could place a Fill-In above a Quick Win (Medium)

ADR 007 §3 rule 5: "Remediation output is ordered by the Risk × Effort matrix, and Fill-Ins never appear above Quick Wins." ADR 004: "The work order reorders by exposure."

reorder_by_exposure implements ADR 004 by re-sorting the whole work order on (-exposure, title), where exposure is 10 × recurrence + churn on the item's path. Band is not in the key. A Fill-In on a file that changes often therefore sorts above a Quick Win on a stable one, which is the nit-loop ADR 007 §3 exists to prevent: low-risk work in the position reserved for the work that matters. D169 made the heading say which sort was applied; it did not make the sort obey the rule.

The two ADRs are compatible as written. ADR 004 requires that exposure reorder the work order; it does not require that exposure outrank band. Decided 2026-09-13: exposure orders items within a band, and band order stays primary.

Population. Every work order built under an economic context, across every band pair ADR 007 §3 ranks.

Filed Open first, from the conflict D169 surfaced, and closed in the same change: the register refuses to carry an Open entry onto main, so the filing is recorded here rather than as a commit of its own.

reorder_by_exposure now sorts on (band, -exposure, title), reading BAND_ORDER — taken from Band's declaration order and shared with the band sort itself, so the two cannot disagree about the matrix. Exposure is untouched within a band. ADR 004 is amended to state "within each band", standard.md says the same, and the heading D169 introduced now reads "ordered by band, then by exposure within each band".

Closing test: test_no_exposure_lifts_a_lower_band_above_a_higher_one in tests/test_exposure_never_outranks_band.py.

Mutation: the sort keeps only Quick Wins above everything else — so the Fill-In-under-Quick-Win pair ADR 007 names still holds — and the test fails on major-project against fill-in and reconsider, pairs the rule does not name.

D171 — Closed: the pre-commit hook worded a declaration unlike the audit it mirrors (High)

From the hostile audit of 77de21c. D164 gave _items_from_hotspots the thresholds so an item names the limit its kind is graded on, and passed them from build_report and check_content. staged_findings — the third caller — was never given them.

A staged 2876-line class blocked the commit with currently 2876 lines; a class is graded on length alone, while the audit of the same class said currently 2876 lines against the 300-line class limit; a function lost its limits and cognitive cost the same way. The hook's own contract is that it writes no remedy text and feeds the audit's builders so a blocked commit reads the report's words. P8 requires a reported value to carry its source; two wordings of one finding from one builder give the reader two.

This was known when D164 closed and was not filed. The D164 session noted that the pre-commit path held no thresholds and would state figures bare, and D164's entry still claimed the wording held on every surface. That is D151's shape — a known gap carried unfiled through a release — and the entry above is amended to say so.

Population. Every declaration the scanners emit, staged through the hook and audited through build_report, compared at the same location.

staged_report now carries the thresholds it graded with, and staged_findings passes them to the builder.

Closing test: test_the_hook_words_every_declaration_as_the_audit_does in tests/test_every_surface_says_what_the_run_found.py.

Mutation: the hook's thresholds stripped of the cognitive limit — which changes function targets, not the class the audit reported — fails the test.

D172 — Closed: the prompt named a source for a maintainability estimate that was not issued (Medium)

The audit's seed run, changed-only over two commits, printed Maintainability estimate: Not scored and, a few lines below, "The maintainability estimate above uses the analyzer readings for declarations".

D163 stopped the prompt issuing rates when the overall is withheld. prompt_analyzer_caveat never consulted whether it was: with analyzer output present it attributed the estimate to the analyzers, or — the branch the audit did not name — said it "comes from the built-in detectors". Both describe the provenance of a number the run declined to produce, which is P8 facing the other way on the artifact D163 was about.

Population. Every state of analyzer_scored_dimensions — none, and each dimension the score document names — on a report whose estimate is withheld.

When is_scored is false, the caveat says no estimate was issued and that analyzer output is evidence about the code, never a change to a score.

Closing test: test_no_prompt_names_a_source_for_an_estimate_that_was_not_issued in tests/test_every_surface_says_what_the_run_found.py.

Mutation: only the analyzer branch gated on is_scored, leaving the built-in-detectors branch — which the audit did not report — fails the test on the no-dimensions state.

D173 — Closed: a run whose report said "Nothing to do" handed the agent a patch to make (High)

A warn-only tree produced no work order. The chat report said "Nothing to do. … there is no prompt to paste". The remediation prompt from the same run opened with "Your task is to fix the highest-value maintainability issues", listed Function hotspots to inspect first, and closed "Make the smallest coherent patch".

The skill tells a chat host to treat remediation_prompt as the bounded work order, so the host patches the warn-band functions the work order refused. On the audit's seed run the evidence note said "must not widen the work order" and the next section listed those functions. P5 requires the prompt to name only work the audit produced; D163's ordering test only runs when there is work, so the empty case was never examined. The same split held for a second case the audit did not name: a work order whose every item is withheld from the prompt — a Major Project, or a finding escalated for design review — still produced the task, the hotspot list and the deliverable.

Population. Both runs with nothing for an agent: an empty work order, and a work order withheld whole.

The "Nothing to do" sentence is one constant, NOTHING_TO_DO, printed by the chat report and the prompt. With nothing in scope, the prompt carries that sentence — or "Nothing in scope for this prompt" and what was withheld — and no task line, hotspot focus or deliverable. The rules stay: they bound what an agent may do whether or not there is work. A run with work is unchanged, and guarded as such. A report with no work_order key at all — stored before the field existed, or assembled by hand — cannot say there is nothing to do and keeps the prompt it always had; the first version of this fix read the missing key as an empty work order, and test_renderers_cover_findings_and_instruction_notes caught it printing "Nothing to do" over failing hotspots. test_class_label_reaches_the_prompt_the_pr_comment_and_sarif asserted a warn-band class label in a prompt with no work; it now gives that prompt one work item, because a warn-only prompt listing hotspots is the defect.

Closing tests: test_a_run_with_no_work_order_hands_the_prompt_no_task and test_a_work_order_withheld_whole_hands_the_prompt_no_task in tests/test_every_surface_says_what_the_run_found.py.

Mutation: "has work" read off the raw work order rather than the items the prompt keeps — right for the empty case the audit reported, wrong for the fully withheld one — fails the second test.

D174 — Closed: a changed-only run handed the prompt a semantic finding from a file the change never touched (Medium)

Changed-only over HEAD~2...HEAD scanned 33 changed paths and nominated tests/fixtures/semantic_ts/src/operations.ts — in none of them — as a semantic candidate in the prompt.

_attach_semantics ran the semantic walk over the whole tree and ignored only_paths. A changed-only report states its scope as the change; a finding from outside it is work the change did not create, handed to an agent told to stay inside it (P5). This predates 3.6.0: the audit's seed run is what made it visible.

Population. Every located semantic finding in a tree that holds the semantic fixture at more than one place, under a change that touches one of them. This repository holds a single located finding — the reported instance — so a population drawn from it would have been the sample.

A changed-only run keeps a semantic finding exactly when its source_evidence.path is in the change; one with no located path could not be placed in or out of it and is not actionable work in any case. A full run is unchanged. The audit also suggested skipping tests/fixtures in the semantic walk; that would change what a full audit examines, which is product intent, and is not done here.

Closing test: test_a_changed_only_run_hands_over_no_semantic_finding_outside_its_paths in tests/test_every_surface_says_what_the_run_found.py.

D175 — Closed: a build changed what the audit scored, because its output was read as the repository's code (High)

Found verifying the demo loop on jhy/jsoup. After mvn test put jsoup's test resources in target/test-classes, the re-audit scanned 261 files instead of 232, gained a failing file (target/test-classes/htmltests/large.html), and doubled the risk class's worth from +0.10 to +0.20 — with no source change behind any of it.

P1 holds the audit deterministic over the same tree. It was deterministic over the working tree and not over the code: one commit scored one way before a build and another after it, and a CI checkout scored differently from the laptop that ran mvn test. ADR 010 exists so that code the team did not write leaves the scored population. The defaults excluded build/, dist/ and node_modules/ by name and not target/, and ADR 010 §1 forbids closing that by adding the name: it is the ggml/ mistake with one more entry.

Two gaps, found in order. The first fix recorded target as generated from the repository's ignore rules and changed nothing measured: discovery classified only files with a source-code suffix, so a generated tree holding HTML, XML and JSON contributed nothing to not_ours and every one of those files stayed scored. That second gap predates D175 — a package.json-rebuilt directory's HTML leaked the same way.

Population. Every suffix the shipped include_extensions reads, placed in an untracked path the repository ignores; and the three things that must stay scored — a tracked file under an ignore rule, an untracked file no rule matches, and a path only a machine's global ignore file excludes.

ignored_untracked_paths asks git, through the hardened run_git, for untracked paths the repository's per-directory .gitignore files exclude — never --exclude-standard, which would read .git/info/exclude and core.excludesFile. Discovery records them as generated with that evidence, and now classifies every file the scanner reads under a generated or vendored tree, not only source. The same built jsoup copy audits at 232 files, 16 file failures and 112 function failures, identical to the unbuilt tree, with the 28 build files counted in summary.generated_files. ADR 010's evidence table is amended.

Closing test: test_ignored_untracked_build_output_leaves_the_score_and_is_counted in tests/test_build_output_is_not_the_repositorys_code.py.

Mutation: only .html classified under a not-ours tree — the suffix of the file the demo exposed — fails the test on the JSON, XML, Markdown and source suffixes the sweep also places there. A second mutation, reading --exclude-standard, fails the global-ignore guard.

D176 — Closed: the chat view and the remediation prompt said nothing about the security pillar, measured or not (High)

Reported from an audit run in the ChatGPT desktop app: MA ran, and the security pillar was not in what came back.

ADR 007 declares the security pillar delegated and reported "so that silence is never read as safety". The JSON report, the complete Markdown and the HTML all carried it. The bounded chat view — the persisted default through the chat door, and so what almost every reader sees — never called pillars_markdown at all, and the remediation prompt had no pillar section. A pillar that was never measured and one that was measured clean rendered identically: as nothing. That is this register's opening theme, capability wired to the TTY, viewer shipped to the primary surface, on the pillar block.

Fixing the display exposed a second fault in it. Every skin explained unverified as "clean scan, but nothing prevents tomorrow's regression" — true for §2's high-condition, low-practice cell, and false for a pillar with no condition at all. secure-code-agent's partial-coverage pillar, practice level 5, rendered as a clean scan. At the base the same misreading applied to every posture carried with no condition.

Population. Every pillar in PILLARS, through the chat view, in each state the security pillar can be in — unmeasured with a reason, measured, and ungraded; the prompt in the same three; and every posture in POSTURE_NOTE with no condition.

The chat view renders the pillar block through the same pillars_markdown the complete report uses, so the two skins cannot disagree about a pillar (ADR 011). The prompt states the security pillar as run evidence — who measured it, posture, practice and condition, or why it was not measured — and names its findings as that tool's work order, not this one's. pillar_cells, shared by Markdown and HTML, says "not graded" and the producer's own reason when no condition exists.

Closing tests: test_every_pillar_reaches_the_chat_view_and_the_prompt and test_an_ungraded_pillar_is_never_described_as_a_clean_scan in tests/test_security_pillar_runs_and_shows.py.

D177 — Closed: the audit did not run the security pillar it declares complete (High)

Amended 2026-09-13 (3.7.2). "secure-code-agent becomes this package's one runtime dependency" is reversed. The range was copied from this project's CI pin (>=0.10,<0.11) while the published tool was at 0.12.1, so installing 3.7.0 downgraded a current machine; and the dependency put secure-code-agent's mcp<2 pin beside this package's mcp>=2 (D178). secure-code-agent's D3 and ADR 008 both hold the tools independently releasable. The audit still runs it on every audit; it now runs whichever release is installed within >=0.12.1,<1, and a missing or unsupported release is the pillar's stated reason with an install command (test_an_unsupported_release_is_stated_not_run).

The same report. Investigating D176 showed the display was only half of it: on the Mac the audit ran on, no measurement existed to display.

The integration built in #208, #213, #214 and #216 was a handoff. secure-code-agent wrote security-pillar.json; this tool read it if it was there. CI runs secure-code-agent before this tool and passes the document, so every pull request carried a measured pillar. Nothing else ran it: not the CLI unless an operator ran the other tool first, and never the chat door, which has no way to pass a document. #214 recorded the same shape — "wired everywhere except where it runs" — and fixed it for CI only. Decided 2026-09-13: the pillar is complete, so the audit runs it. ADR 007 is amended.

Population. An audit of a real repository with no document handed over; a repository that plants its own security-pillar.json; an operator who names one; and an interpreter without secure-code-agent installed.

_security_delegate runs secure-code-agent through _runner.run — rule 7's single execution path — with the auditing interpreter (D144, D166) and a neutral working directory. Every report the tool can write is redirected to a temporary directory. Its append-only .secure-code/history.jsonl cannot be redirected without handing it a configuration from outside the tree, and secure-code-agent trusts an outside configuration to name executables inside the tree, so that override would launder repository content past the other tool's own boundary: the history is declared as this tool's sixth write instead, in server_info and the MCP instructions. An operator's --security-pillar document is used as given and the tool is not run again. A document the audited tree supplies is no longer read on its own authority: a repository may not report its own security posture in place of a measurement. secure-code-agent becomes this package's one runtime dependency; not installed, a rejected configuration or a timeout is the pillar's stated reason, and a missing install is a line in the environment work order.

Found running it on this machine: bighound's secure-code-agent.json carries a _comment key that secure-code-agent 0.10.0 rejects, so its pillar reads "exited 2 without a pillar document" with that message. That is the repository's configuration, reported as the reason, not a fault here.

Closing tests: test_an_audit_runs_secure_code_agent_and_leaves_only_its_declared_history, test_a_pillar_document_the_tree_supplies_is_not_reported_as_the_measurement and test_a_missing_delegate_is_stated_with_its_remedy in tests/test_security_pillar_runs_and_shows.py. The last fails at the base by import rather than by assertion, because the module it patches did not exist.

D178 — Closed: a documented scanner install broke the chat door, and the door's error called an installed package missing (High)

Found proving D177 through a fresh MCP server. maintainability-agent-mcp exited at startup: ImportError: cannot import name 'MCPServer', reported as "MCP support is not installed. Install with: pip install "maintainability-agent[mcp]"".

mcp was installed — at 1.23.3. Installing secure-code-agent's scanner extras into this package's environment, as the 3.7.0 docs/machine-setup.md told a reader to, pulled in njsscan and semgrep; semgrep pins mcp<2; pip uninstalled this package's mcp 2.0.0 to satisfy it. pip check reported nothing, because the mcp requirement lives in the mcp extra, which it does not inspect. The session already running kept working, so the break would have surfaced at the next restart — of this editor's chat door, and of any host sharing the environment.

Two defects. The documentation prescribed an install that breaks the product (P5's sibling for docs: an instruction with no basis in what actually works). And the startup message described an installed package as absent and printed, as its remedy, the requirement the conflicting package had just overridden (P8): the reader learned neither the version present nor what installed it.

Population. The three states mcp's metadata can be in when the import fails — absent, outside >=2,<3, and inside it but unimportable — at the function and at create_server, the seam the operator meets.

mcp_unavailable_message reads the installed version and the distributions that require mcp. Absent keeps the install instruction. Out of range names the version, the requirement and the dependents, and says to give that tool its own environment. In range reports the import error itself rather than a version it does not have. The setup page installs bandit on its own and semgrep and checkov from Homebrew, and states why the extras must not share this environment.

Closing tests: test_a_version_outside_the_range_names_it_and_who_required_it, test_a_version_inside_the_range_reports_the_import_error_itself and test_the_console_entry_raises_the_named_message in tests/test_mcp_start_failure_names_what_is_installed.py.

Mutation: the dependents found by a prefix test on the requirement string — the shortcut the first draft took — blames mcp-helper, a distribution the sweep plants that requires mcpx and not mcp, and fails the out-of-range test.

D180 — Closed: the prompt withheld a finding as a design decision while its copy-paste block told an agent to make the change (High)

D182 — Closed: the supported secure-code-agent release ignored reviewed paths: suppressions on every audit this tool runs (High)

Found closing D179. That fixed this repository's report. It left every other repository's paths: entries broken.

The defect was secure-code-agent's, and it was fixed there as its D20 in 0.12.2, released 2026-09-14. What stayed wrong here was the range: SUPPORTED_FLOOR still admitted 0.12.1, which writes the same schema v2 document and so passes every check this audit makes of the contract.

Reproduced on this repository, audited from a clone with the paths: entry restored:

secure-code-agent gitleaks.curl-auth-user findings suppressed
0.12.1 4 0
0.12.2 4 4

The floor is now 0.12.2. The requirement in the environment work order, the five CI pins, the README, CONTRIBUTING, the machine-setup page and ADR 007 say the same thing, and .scignore.yaml is back to paths: for both entries.

The floor, the install requirement and the CI pins stay in step through two guards that already existed and pass at the 3.7.4 base: test_the_supported_range_and_the_install_remedy_are_one_declaration and test_every_ci_pin_is_a_release_the_audit_runs in tests/test_security_delegate_versions_agree.py. D183 — Closed: an address outside the commit identity reached history through commit messages and file content (High)

The identity check read two fields, and the address came in through two others.

The authorship workflow required every commit's author and committer to be the repository's commit identity, and a history rewrite had removed the commits that failed it. Both were checked. Neither covered the message or the files: a personal address that must never be attributed to this project survived in Co-authored-by: trailers on five squash-merged commits, in the body of a sixth, and in the content of two tracked documentation and workflow files across 159 historical commits. Each release published in that window carried it in its source archive.

It was removed by rewriting every branch and tag, deleting the 47 releases whose tags GitHub will not let move, and deleting the CI runs whose logs printed it. None of that stops the next occurrence, which is this entry.

Two steps are added to the already-required authorship job, so they are enforced from the day they merge:

  • Commit messages name only the commit identity. Any address in a commit message, merges included, other than the repository's commit identity fails the pull request. It is an allowlist, so it catches a trailer for any address rather than a list of known ones.
  • No forbidden term in the change. Terms that must never appear live in a repository secret, not in the tree, so the check does not publish what it guards. It reads each commit's identity fields and message and every added line, reports a match by commit or file without printing the text, and fails closed when the secret is unset.

Closing tests: test_a_trailer_naming_another_address_fails, test_a_forbidden_term_in_a_message_fails_in_any_case, test_a_forbidden_term_added_to_a_file_fails_without_printing_it and test_the_forbidden_term_check_fails_closed_without_the_secret in tests/test_authorship_gates.py.

Mutation: the trailer check narrowed back to --no-merges passes every named case and lets a merge message carry an address, which is outside the sample those tests name; the term check reading only the diff and not the message fails the message case; dropping the empty-secret guard turns the unset secret into a clean pass and fails the fail-closed case.

D184 — Closed: the security pillar could not be graded, because the delegate config asserted no scanner set (Medium)

Coverage was complete and the grade was still withheld.

secure-code-agent releases a security grade only when the configuration names the scanners that must have run. This repository's delegate config declared fail_on_severity, fail_on_category, fail_on_new and max_unsuppressed, and never require_scanners. Every audit therefore reported a posture of unverified — not because anything was wrong with the code, and not because a scanner had failed, but because nothing had been asserted to have run. The one signal was a line in the delegate's own output: "grade withheld: no gates.require_scanners is declared, so no scanner set was asserted to have run".

It was read as the other half of the same symptom for some time. The other half was real and is fixed in secure-code-agent 0.12.5 (its D23): osv-scanner exits 128 with "No package sources found" on a repository with no lockfile, which that adapter mapped to a scanner failure, holding coverage at partial permanently. Fixing it took this repository's coverage to complete and left the grade withheld anyway, which is what isolated this defect from that one.

gates.require_scanners now names bandit, builtin_rules, pip_audit and njsscan. The set is deliberately the delegate's own required-scanners extra: those install from PyPI, so the runner that asserts them can have them. Requiring gitleaks, semgrep or trivy would fail every pull request, since they are binaries CI does not install; requiring osv_scanner would fail for the reason its own fix makes explicit — a repository with no package sources cannot satisfy a scanner that reads package sources.

The two halves are held together rather than separately: the job that runs the gate installs secure-code-agent[required-scanners], because naming a scanner the runner lacks converts complete coverage into a hard gate failure on the absence of a tool rather than on anything about the code.

Closing tests: test_the_delegate_config_asserts_a_scanner_set and test_the_job_that_runs_the_gate_installs_the_scanners_it_requires in tests/test_security_pillar_asserts_a_scanner_set.py.

Mutation: removing require_scanners from the config restores the withheld grade and fails the first test; installing the delegate in the gate job without its required-scanners extra passes the first and fails the second, which is the shape that would otherwise reach CI as a gate failure blamed on the code.

D185 — Closed: consent to run the test suite, with no command recorded, was a dead end no surface named a way out of (Medium)

Answering "yes" and getting silence.

test_execution.requested true with no expected_commands.test in the user tier is a reachable state: setup records the consent and does not record the program. D147 and D152 make the refusal correct — the tree may say how it is tested, only the person may choose what this host executes — but the person is then left with a suite that never runs and nothing telling them why it is fixable.

Neither door said anything useful. The report's detail explained the refusal and named no remedy. The MCP gate's discovery line is guarded by run_tests_pending, which is true only when test_execution is absent — the config that predates the opt-in — so a config that answered the question read as asked-and-answered and got no line either.

This repository's own audit sat in that state. Every self-audit for weeks reported "the operator opted in to running the repository's test command; it did not run", and the testability reading was taken without a suite execution behind it.

The detail now names the remedy, and consented_without_command() gives the gate the predicate run_tests_pending cannot express, so the chat door offers reconfigure for this state as it already does for the other one.

Closing tests: test_the_report_detail_names_a_remedy, test_the_predicate_sees_consent_without_a_command, test_a_recorded_command_is_not_reported_as_missing and test_the_mcp_gate_offers_reconfigure_for_the_gap in tests/test_consent_without_command_has_a_remedy.py.

Mutation: restoring the detail string without the remedy sentence fails the first; making the predicate read the merged config rather than the user tier passes the second and fails the third, which is the D147 inversion it exists to refuse; removing the elif branch at the gate fails the fourth while the report-side tests still pass, which is the shape that let this survive.

D186 — Closed: the security pillar was held at B+ by findings that are what this tool is (Medium)

Sixty-two reports of a subprocess, all correct, all permanent.

D184 gave the pillar a scanner set to assert, so it could carry a grade. The grade it carried was 3.82 (B+), and reading what held it there found nothing to fix: 62 bandit findings for importing and calling subprocess, in a tool whose entire purpose is to invoke other analyzers, plus three for reading Checkstyle and JUnit XML this repository did not write, two for calling the SonarCloud API, and one for a seeded bootstrap in the calibration study.

Every one is a true report about an architecture that is not going to change. The instruments available were exclude_patterns and --skip, which delete the observation and leave no trace — the silence this tool's own _conformance detector counts as a defect when it finds NOSONAR in someone else's tree — or an annually expiring suppression per finding, which is a ritual re-approving a fact that has not changed.

secure-code-agent 0.12.6 added capabilities (its D24) and 0.12.7 made the stated reason print on the page (its D25). The delegate config now declares four, each with the sentence a reviewer reads to decide it is still true, and the findings stay in the report on their own axes: counted, listed, not scored. The summary discloses both numbers — 5.00 (A+) with the declarations and without declarations 3.82 (B+) beside it — so the movement is stated rather than silent.

Four bandit B105 findings in _masking.py are not declared. They are a name heuristic firing on the word token in a JavaScript tokenizer, which is a false positive and not an architectural shape, so they are acknowledged in .scignore.yaml with a reason and an expiry — the instrument that fits them.

Declaring also couples this repository to a delegate release. Below 0.12.6 secure-code-agent's strict unknown-key sweep refuses the whole configuration, so the audit does not run; 0.12.6 runs it and prints no reason under the declared axes. SUPPORTED_FLOOR moved from 0.12.2 to 0.12.7 for that reason, and the five CI pins with it.

Closing tests: test_every_declared_capability_is_one_the_delegate_defines, test_every_declaration_states_a_reason, test_declaring_capabilities_raised_the_supported_floor and test_no_ci_pin_predates_the_disclosing_release in tests/test_declared_capabilities_match_the_delegate.py. The gate on this repository passes at 0 scored findings with COMPLETE coverage, and secure-code-report.md prints each declaration's reason under its axis.

The known-name set in that file is copied from the delegate rather than imported: the two tools are independently releasable and this suite does not import secure-code-agent, which ADR 008 requires and the declared-imports sweep enforces. What guards the copy is the delegate itself, which refuses a capability name it does not define at config load — a drifted name fails the audit rather than passing quietly. The local set catches the typo in the suite instead of in CI.

Mutation: inventing a capability name the delegate does not define routes nothing while reading as a decision, and fails the first; a declaration with a token reason fails the second; leaving the floor at 0.12.2 fails the third while every other test in the file still passes, and is the state where this repository's own config stops its audit from running at all; leaving one pin at 0.12.6 fails the fourth — the findings route and no reason prints, which is the grade moved in silence that declaring was supposed to replace.

D187 — Closed: the full security record was uploaded to code scanning, so every assert in a test blocked the merge (Medium)

Seventeen threads, all of them assert.

Found trying to merge the pull request that closed D186. Every required check passed and the merge was refused: branch protection requires every review thread resolved, and GitHub Advanced Security had opened seventeen of them from the uploaded SARIF — one per assert in the three test files the branch added.

secure-code-agent writes two SARIF documents for exactly this reason (its D22, which records the same failure in its own repository: fourteen threads, twelve for assert in pytest). --sarif-output is the record — every result, a suppressed one carrying its suppressions entry and reason. Code scanning does not honour that array: it opens an alert for every result it is handed, which is why GitHub ships a separate action to dismiss them afterwards. --code-scanning-sarif-output is the same document without the suppressed results.

This repository uploaded the record. The test tree is reported-not-scored in the pillar and in the report, and that separation stopped at the one door where it decided whether a human could merge. It is not a one-off: it is the outcome for every pull request that adds a test file, permanently.

The audit now writes both documents, code scanning gets the filtered one, and the record is still kept as a build artifact. Nothing is hidden — the record, the JSON report, the Markdown report and the work order all still carry every finding. What changed is which document becomes somebody's blocking alert.

Closing tests: test_the_security_record_is_never_uploaded_to_code_scanning, test_the_filtered_document_is_what_is_uploaded, test_the_audit_asks_the_delegate_to_write_the_filtered_document and test_the_filtered_document_is_not_committed in tests/test_code_scanning_gets_alerts_not_the_record.py.

Mutation: restoring sarif_file: secure-code.sarif fails the first; removing the upload entirely passes the first and fails the second, which is why proving the record absent is not enough; dropping the --code-scanning-sarif-output flag while keeping the upload passes both and fails the third, and is the state where the upload step names a file nothing wrote; leaving the new document out of .gitignore fails the fourth and commits a per-run artifact.

D188 — Closed: the agent reported its own version from source, so a stale process could never notice it was stale (Medium)

"3.7.7", stated plainly, all day, with nothing marking it wrong.

config.VERSION is a source constant. Every surface reports it as agent_version, so the number is read from whatever code is loaded and is therefore always self-consistent: it cannot be wrong about itself. A server started before an upgrade answers every call with its own old version as fact, and a reader has no way to tell that from a correct answer.

That is what happened here on 2026-09-18. 3.7.8 shipped, the MCP server kept serving 3.7.7 from a process started the previous day, and it said so on every gate reply without comment. The audit that would have reported D184–D187 was running code that predated them.

Underneath it was a second, worse state. The venv actually serving MCP was an editable install whose dist-info froze at 1.8.0 while the source tree had moved to 3.7.8, and it carried secure-code-agent metadata at 0.2.0 against a supported floor of 0.12.7. --version reads the source and printed the right answer throughout.

This is not cosmetic, and the reason is this repository's own code: _security_delegate gates the delegate on metadata.version("secure-code-agent"). A frozen environment does not merely misreport a number — it refuses a correctly installed delegate and reports the Security pillar unmeasured while every other signal reads green. The audit was strict about the sibling's installed version and blind to its own.

_running_version closes the asymmetry. The installed distribution's metadata is a second opinion, written to disk by the installer and read fresh, so the two disagree exactly when something is out of date — and which side is ahead names the fault and therefore the remedy:

  • loaded code behind the metadata is a stale process, and the remedy is to restart, not reinstall;
  • loaded code ahead of it is a frozen editable install, and the remedy is to reinstall, because restarting changes nothing.

Reported on every surface that reports a version: the gate replies that run no audit (where this hid), the audit result, server_info, and the CLI on stderr before it prints anything else — because it is about whether to trust what the run is about to say.

Silent when the two agree, and silent when metadata cannot be read at all: a source checkout with nothing installed is a legitimate way to run this, and a check that fired on every one of those runs would be trained away inside a day and unread on the day it mattered.

Closing tests: test_agreement_reports_nothing, test_an_uninstalled_source_checkout_reports_nothing, test_a_stale_process_is_named_as_a_stale_process, test_a_frozen_editable_install_is_named_as_one, test_the_editable_remedy_names_what_else_reads_the_stale_number, test_an_unorderable_version_still_reports_the_disagreement, test_the_gate_reply_carries_the_drift, test_the_gate_reply_is_unchanged_when_versions_agree and test_server_info_states_the_drift_field in tests/test_the_agent_knows_when_it_is_stale.py.

Mutation: comparing the two versions for inequality without ordering them passes every mismatch test and fails the two that separate a stale process from a frozen editable install — the whole value of the check, since the remedies are opposite and one of them fixes nothing; returning a drift when metadata is unreadable fails the source-checkout test and produces the warning-on-every-run that gets ignored; dropping the version_drift call from _envelope leaves every unit test green while restoring exactly the surface that went stale unnoticed; removing the delegate sentence from the editable remedy passes the rest and leaves a reader believing a frozen dist-info is a display bug.

D189 — Closed: the setup questions could be asked but not answered, on every host that cannot be elicited (High)

Seven questions, asked twice, with nowhere to put the answers.

Found by a user reconfiguring this repository through the chat door. The tool returned setup_needed with its seven questions, they were put to him as structured choices, he answered all seven — and the configuration on disk was untouched. Calling again returned choice_needed, as though nothing had been asked. He answered eleven questions across two attempts and every one was discarded.

The mechanism. audit_repository_tool takes setup, annotated Annotated[ElicitationResult[Any], resolve(setup_resolver)]: an elicitation-resolved parameter the MCP SDK fills by asking the host. Being SDK-resolved, it is absent from the tool's published input schema, so no client can pass it. _mcp_setup's own docstring describes the degradation path — "a host that declines, or cannot elicit at all, gets the same questions back as data, so its own question UI can ask and call again" — and that path was never implemented. Calling again returns the same questions. The loop cannot close.

This is D185's shape again, one door over: a state a user can reach, be asked about, and have no way out of. D185 closed the morning of the same day.

Why review did not find it, including an auditor's. In source the path reads correctly: the function takes setup, _apply_call_consents reads it, apply_answers writes the file, and every line is right. The defect exists only in the difference between the function's signature and the schema published to a caller, which is a separate artifact. Reading the code cannot show it; only calling the tool as a client can.

The fix, and the class. setup_answers: dict[str, str] | None is an ordinary published parameter carrying the same answers to the same apply_answers, so an elicited host and a data host now travel one road. The legal names are derived from setup_questions rather than listed beside them, so a question added without a way to answer it is not expressible. A key no question asked for is refused rather than dropped, because apply_answers ignores names it does not know — right for a validated elicitation payload, wrong for an argument a host typed, where dept would persist the default depth and report success.

The gate is test_every_setup_question_has_a_submittable_name, which asserts against the caller-facing surface with the SDK-resolved parameters removed — the artifact the defect lived in, rather than the source that looked fine.

Closing tests: test_every_setup_question_has_a_submittable_name, test_the_submittable_names_are_derived_from_the_questions, test_the_tool_publishes_a_parameter_that_carries_setup_answers, test_the_elicitation_parameter_is_not_counted_as_an_answer_path, test_submitted_answers_reach_the_local_configuration, test_a_misspelled_answer_is_refused_rather_than_dropped and test_the_answers_a_host_is_shown_are_all_accepted in tests/test_every_question_can_be_answered.py.

Mutation: counting setup as an answer path makes the publishing test pass in exactly the state that shipped, which is why the SDK-resolved names are removed and a second test holds that removal; replacing the derived name set with a literal list passes every test here and reintroduces the drift the day a question is added; dropping _refuse_unknown_answers passes all seven and returns a success for a misspelled key that silently persisted a default; asserting the applied return value rather than the file on disk passes against the broken path too, since it reported success while writing nothing.

D190 — Closed: only the elicited host was told which setup stage it was in (Medium)

Answered stage one, which opened stage two, and was handed stage one again.

Setup is staged. economics: include opens the three labor rates; run_tests: yes opens the test command. The second stages exist so nobody is asked for a rate they do not want to give.

setup_schema knew that. The gate returning questions as data did not: it called setup_questions(load_config(None)) — no root, so no way to know the stage — and could only ever produce stage one. A host that cannot be elicited answered the seven, which set economic_context.requested, which opened stage two, and the next reply was the same seven questions. The rates were unreachable from that surface.

Found immediately after D189, by using the thing D189 fixed: the answers now landed, and the follow-up question never came.

This is D189's asymmetry one layer in. There the answers had nowhere to go; here they went somewhere and the next question never arrived. Both are one shape — the elicited path knew something the data path did not — and D189's gate did not catch this because it asked "can every question be answered", never "are the right questions being asked".

staged_questions is now the single stage decision. setup_schema renders it as an elicitation model and the gate returns it as data; two renderings, never two opinions.

Closing tests: test_both_paths_offer_the_same_questions (parametrised over the economics, suite and neither states), test_opting_into_economics_moves_the_data_path_to_the_rates, test_a_repository_needing_nothing_staged_is_asked_the_first_stage, test_the_gate_reply_carries_the_staged_questions and test_the_staged_questions_are_answerable in tests/test_both_setup_paths_ask_the_same_stage.py.

Mutation: testing parity in only one repository state passes against the broken code in two of the three, because stage one is what both paths produce when nothing is staged — which is why the parity test is parametrised over every staging state; asserting the data path only by agreement with the schema passes if both regress together, which is why the rates are also asserted positively; leaving the gate calling setup_questions directly passes every test that inspects the helper and fails the one that reads the reply a host receives.

D191 — Closed: the tool wrote a configuration its own published schema rejected (Medium)

Four blocks setup writes, none of them described.

maintainability-agent.schema.json is what an operator points their editor and their CI at. It sets additionalProperties: false and described ten blocks. First-run setup writes four more — presentation, history, test_execution and economic_context — so completing setup produced a file the tool's own contract called invalid, flagged on every line of it by any editor honouring the $schema link.

Found by the schema-fidelity suite the moment this repository's config gained those blocks. It had passed for the life of the schema because the only configuration it validated was this repository's own, which predated setup and carried none of them. A fixture that cannot reach the broken state is a test that cannot fail.

The gate is now the round trip rather than a list: run apply_answers and validate what lands on disk, across every answer combination and both staged second asks. A settings block added without a schema entry fails here instead of in somebody's editor.

Writing that gate immediately caught a second error — in the fix itself. The rates were assumed to persist as labor_low/labor_base/labor_high at the top of the block; they are actually nested under loaded_engineering_cost_per_hour, and requested is replaced rather than kept. A hand-written schema entry would have shipped that wrong and passed a list-based check.

Closing tests: test_a_completed_setup_validates_against_the_published_schema, test_every_block_setup_writes_is_described_by_the_schema, test_every_answer_combination_writes_a_valid_config (parametrised over the declined options) and test_the_staged_second_answers_also_validate in tests/test_setup_writes_a_valid_config.py. This repository's own config is already validated by the schema-fidelity suite, which is what caught this; duplicating that check here would have defended nothing and is deliberately absent.

Mutation: validating a hand-written fixture instead of the round trip passes with any schema that happens to match the fixture, which is exactly how this survived; testing only the all-yes answers misses the shapes a declined option writes; omitting the staged second call misses the nested rate block entirely, which is the shape the first attempt at this fix got wrong.

D192 — Closed: the setup instruction described a different reply than the one it shipped with (Low)

Setup "has not been set up", above three questions that exist because it was.

setup_instruction is the prose an agent relaying the questions reads. It was written once and served every stage. A stage-two reply — the three labor rates, reachable only by answering stage one — opened by saying setup had never happened, then recited the option list for default_format with no such question anywhere in the payload.

Neither costs an answer, so neither is a dead end. They cost the instruction its authority. It is the only thing telling a host not to invent questions of its own and not to answer on the user behalf, and a reader who finds it contradicting the payload beside it stops reading it — at which point the substituted-question failure it exists to prevent is back.

It also never named setup_answers. It said "call audit_repository again" and stopped, which was the whole truth available before D189 and is now a missing sentence: the host is told to call again and not told what to put in the call.

The text is derived from the questions in the reply. The opening names the stage, the presentation options appear only where default_format is actually asked, and every stage says how to submit.

Closing tests: test_a_staged_reply_does_not_claim_setup_never_happened, test_a_staged_reply_does_not_recite_the_presentation_options and test_every_stage_says_how_to_submit_the_answers (parametrised over all three stages) in tests/test_setup_instruction_describes_its_own_reply.py.

The first-stage wording is held separately, in tests/test_first_stage_setup_wording_holds.py. Those assertions pass at the base commit: they defend this change rather than prove it, and the file says so. Citing them here would make this entry look better attested than it is.

Mutation: dropping the presentation recital entirely passes the staged test and fails the first-stage one, which is the regression that would let a host offer two of the three presentations — the D26 failure that recital exists to prevent; naming the stage from the reconfigure flag rather than from the questions passes on a first run and restores the false opening on every staged reply.

D193 — Closed: submitting the answers was published, but not finished (Medium)

From a Grok audit on 178a642, verified by tests driving a real client.

D189 published setup_answers and D190 staged the questions behind it. Both were checked with helpers called directly. Driving an actual MCP client through list_tools() and a full round trip found the path still unusable in three ways, and a fourth thing wrong beside it.

The rates never landed. The published parameter is a mapping of strings — what a JSON schema can say about a flat object a host fills in — and the rates are stored as numbers. "90" stayed a string, _is_bounds_only did not recognise the reply, the bounds stayed pending, and the host was asked the same question forever. The earlier tests passed floats straight to apply_answers and never crossed the published boundary where the conversion is owed.

An illegal value was accepted. apply_answers reads the vocabulary it knows and defaults the rest, which is right for an elicitation payload the SDK validated against a schema and wrong for an argument a host typed: depth: "nuclear" persisted moderate and reported success. A submission was silently answered as something the user did not choose. D189 refused unknown keys and never examined values.

Three surfaces said "call again" without saying how. SERVER_INSTRUCTIONS and both SKILL.md copies carried the sentence that was the whole truth before D189 and is now an omission. D192 fixed setup_instruction and stopped there, which is the same half-fix in a smaller frame.

coerce_submitted_answers does keys, values and types from one reading of published_questions, so a submission is judged against exactly what the host was offered. A question added anywhere is validated everywhere without a second list.

And the description came back inline. D192 moved the tool description to a module constant to keep _audit_tool_for inside the function-length gate. The description is the published contract and the test that holds it reads the source, so a constant is a second text to drift from the first. The closure body moves to _run_audit_tool instead: the gate is satisfied by moving code, not by separating a contract from the thing it describes.

Closing tests: test_submitted_economics_and_test_answers_advance_the_derived_stages, test_host_submissions_refuse_values_outside_the_published_questions (parametrised over depth and default_format) and test_every_call_again_instruction_names_the_public_answer_path in tests/test_setup_answers_are_the_same_setup.py, written by Codex against the published surface rather than the helpers behind it.

The fourth test in that suite, which asserts list_tools() serves setup_answers and none of the private resolver parameters, guards D189 and passes at this entry's base. It is kept — the defect it holds is invisible in the source, since the function has always taken a setup parameter — and lives in tests/test_list_tools_publishes_the_answer_path.py with its assertions unchanged, so it is not counted as evidence for this entry.

Mutation: validating submissions against a hand-written vocabulary passes every value test and drifts the day a question changes its options, which is the second-source-of-truth shape D189 and D190 were both instances of; refusing an illegal value after apply_answers has run leaves the file written, which is why the check happens before anything is persisted; keeping the tool description in a module constant passes a test that reads __doc__ and fails the one that reads the source, and the source is what a reviewer checks.

D194 — Closed: an agent could serve a presentation the user did not choose, and nothing said so (Medium)

presentation.format = "html" in the config; format: "json" in the result; no key connecting the two.

Found by the operator asking where his HTML report was. The relaying agent had passed format="json" for its own convenience while telling him it was running his persisted default, and the result gave him no way to catch it: format names what was produced and nothing names what was chosen.

The override itself is correct and stays. CI asks for json and must receive json; the tool has always documented format as a per-call value that beats the persisted one. What was wrong is that it beat it silently, which makes "the user asked for json" and "the agent preferred json" the same result.

This is D26's failure with the report produced instead of withheld. There a user never saw the html report because the question was handed over as data and never asked; here the question was asked, answered, and the answer overridden on the way out.

It was also the only divergence this tool did not disclose. Coverage says reported-not-scored and why. Pillars say not-measured and why. The environment work order names what could not run. The delegate prints the score its declarations moved. Presentation was the gap, and presentation is the one an agent can widen on its own.

presentation_override: {requested, persisted} appears only when a call names a format that differs from the recorded one, so it stays a signal rather than a field on every reply.

Closing tests: test_a_different_format_than_the_user_chose_is_disclosed and test_every_presentation_discloses_the_same_way (parametrised over every presentation that is not the recorded one) in tests/test_a_format_override_is_disclosed.py.

The silent cases — an honoured preference, an unset call, a repository that recorded no choice, and the override still taking effect — are held by tests/test_a_matching_format_stays_silent.py. They pass at this entry's base and are not cited: they defend the change rather than prove it.

Mutation: refusing the override instead of disclosing it breaks the CI door and fails the second test, which is why disclosure and not refusal is the fix; emitting the key on every run passes the disclosure tests and fails the three that require silence, and is the always-on alarm nobody reads; comparing against the chat fallback rather than the persisted value reports an override on every unconfigured repository, where there is no choice to displace.

D196 — Closed: the audit said which program was configured, never which one ran (Medium)

One configuration, two surfaces, opposite answers, and nothing saying why.

Through the CLI the consented pytest ran 2,908 tests and measured 96% coverage. Through the MCP server the same command exited 3, the suite did not run, and test_effectiveness was null — so one door graded test effectiveness and the other could not, from the same config on the same machine in the same minute.

The mechanism is locate, and it is working as designed. It searches the agent's own script directory before PATH so a virtualenv finds the analyzers installed beside it (D142). The consequence is that resolution depends on which interpreter is running the agent. The MCP server here was a venv console script invoked by a foreign interpreter, so sys.executable was that interpreter and locate("pytest") returned ~/Library/Python/3.11/bin/pytest — a different environment's pytest, which could not import the project. The CLI, running under the venv's own interpreter, resolved .venv/bin/pytest.

Nothing in either result showed that. command is the name the person consented to; what the host executed was never reported, so the two runs looked identical and disagreed. Finding it took six probes and two wrong diagnoses from me — a resolved path in the result would have made it one look.

This is D194's shape. The tool substituted something correctly and by design, and did not say what it substituted. resolved_program is now carried on every ToolResult that reached an exec, and surfaced in test_suite beside the configured command.

The foreign-interpreter launch is an environment fault and is fixed separately on the operator's machine. This entry is about the audit being unable to tell anyone it happened.

Closing tests: test_a_run_reports_the_program_it_resolved, test_an_unresolvable_program_is_reported_as_given, test_the_field_defaults_to_none_for_results_that_never_executed, test_the_suite_result_names_the_program and test_a_suite_that_never_ran_still_carries_the_key in tests/test_the_suite_names_the_program_it_ran.py.

Mutation: reporting invocation.argv[0] rather than the resolved path satisfies "the result names a program" and reports the bare name that was already there, which is the state this entry describes; omitting the key on the paths that never reached an exec makes a consumer guess whether the field is missing or the program unresolved; setting it only on the success path leaves the failing run — the one a reader is investigating — silent about what it tried to execute.

D197 — Closed: the HTML report quoted the security work order as Markdown source (Medium)

An operator chose HTML and got ## headings inside the page.

complete_html wrapped the delegate's work order in <pre> and its docstring said so: "the same text, preformatted". In a Markdown report that is invisible. In the HTML report — the presentation a person chooses when they want something readable — it is a hundred lines of raw source in a monospace box, headings as hashes, bold as asterisks, in a page where everything else is rendered.

Reported by the operator on sight, which is the only way it could have been found: every test asserted the text was carried, and it was.

The fix is not a Markdown parser. Prose is the one thing this tool cannot re-present, and parsing another tool's prose to draw it would be worse than the symptom. secure-code-agent now emits the same work order as facts (its D27), --work-order-json carries them, and the HTML draws them.

Still the delegate's work order. ADR 007 §1 says this tool does not re-rank, merge or reword security findings, so the data is drawn in the delegate's own tiers, order and counts, with its caps and its omitted number. A different presentation of the same work, never a different opinion about it.

An older delegate is still readable. No data means the Markdown is preformatted exactly as before — the honest rendering of prose, and better than a blank section. The floor moves to 0.12.8 because that is where the promise is kept, not because anything crashes below it.

Writing the ingestion surfaced a second error in the fix itself: the work-order JSON was first read with read_produced, which is the pillar reader and refuses any document lacking the pillar's own schema key. It returned None for a file written correctly, and the report fell back to quoting Markdown with nothing saying why — the same silent-fallback shape this register keeps recording. It has its own reader now, checking its own contract.

Closing tests: test_markdown_syntax_does_not_reach_the_page, test_a_finding_is_rendered_as_elements, test_the_code_snippet_stays_preformatted, test_the_citation_is_drawn, test_the_delegate_is_named_as_the_author, test_the_counts_are_the_delegate_s_own, test_a_capped_tier_says_how_many_are_missing, test_a_review_finding_shows_why_it_was_demoted, and test_without_data_the_markdown_is_still_carried in tests/test_the_security_work_order_is_drawn.py.

The empty case — a report with no work order renders no section — is held by tests/test_an_absent_work_order_renders_nothing.py. It passes at this entry's base and is not cited: it defends the change rather than proving it.

Mutation: asserting only that the findings appear passes against the <pre> that shipped, since the text was always there — which is why the first test asserts the Markdown syntax is absent; dropping the no-data path renders a blank section for a supported older release; re-counting the findings here instead of printing the delegate's counts passes every rendering test and makes this tool a second opinion on security, which ADR 007 §1 forbids.

D198 — Closed: the version was written in five places, and a release failed because one was missed (Medium)

The most coupled pair in this repository's own audit, and it was a copy.

change_coupling scored 2.0, the joint-lowest aspect. The top pair was __init__.py and config.py at 64 co-changes, 0.985 confidence — higher than any pair of modules that actually depend on each other. They were coupled because every release edited both, along with pyproject.toml and two documents: five files to change one number.

That is not a tidiness complaint. v3.7.9 was tagged and failed its release because four of the five were updated and the release plan's row was not. And secure-code-agent shipped a wheel stamped with one version while every SARIF document, JSON report and --version it produced carried another, because its pyproject.toml held a second copy that had drifted. PyPI is immutable, so that release stayed wrong.

The obvious fix is a test asserting the copies agree. That is the wrong fix, and this project's sibling wrote down why: such a test polices the duplication rather than removing it, and leaves the failure mode in place for the day someone adds a sixth copy.

One literal. maintainability_audit.__version__ holds it. config.VERSION re-exports that object rather than restating it — is, not ==, because an equal copy is exactly what was removed. pyproject.toml declares dynamic = ["version"] and reads the attribute, so the wheel cannot be stamped with anything else.

The two documents that also carry the number — the README header and the release plan's "Last tagged version" row — stay prose. They are already gated by their own tests, and they are now the only places a human types it.

D85's agreement check was retired with its premise rather than left passing vacuously; that entry is amended above to say so.

The first attempt broke the release it was fixing. release.yml compares the tag against the packaged version by reading pyproject's static version key — one of the copies this entry removed. v3.7.17 was tagged and failed eleven seconds in with KeyError: 'version'. The gate protecting against a mis-stamped release was itself reading a duplicate, which is the same defect one level up. It reads the literal now.

Closing tests: test_config_reexports_rather_than_duplicating, test_config_holds_no_version_literal, test_pyproject_derives_the_version, test_the_dynamic_version_points_at_the_package, test_only_one_module_writes_the_number and test_the_release_gate_reads_the_single_source in tests/test_the_version_has_one_source.py.

Mutation: asserting VERSION == __version__ rather than is passes with the copy restored, which is the check this replaces; re-exporting while also keeping a literal in config.py passes the identity test and fails the literal sweep; declaring dynamic while pointing the attribute at a different module passes the dynamic test and fails the one that names the package; a sweep over a hand-typed list of modules misses the sixth holder, which is the shape that produced this entry.

D199 — Closed: the chat view reported a suite-derived value and never said the suite had run (Medium)

test effectiveness: not measurable, for a suite that ran and failed.

D196 made the audit record which program it executed. The skins then disagreed about it: the complete Markdown and HTML named the resolved program, the bounded chat view named nothing — because it never rendered the test suite at all.

That is not a missing section. The chat view still prints test effectiveness and a testability score in its tables, so a reader was given a value and no way to recover what produced it. P8's violation is stated in exactly those words: "a reported value with no attributable source."

Worse than absent: wrong. A suite that ran and failed was reported as not measurable. It was measured. The measurement was a failure, and the reader was told no measurement existed.

This is D176 again, one artifact over. That entry put the pillars into the chat view because "the chat view printed nothing about security at all, measured or not, and a missing pillar read exactly like a clean one". A missing suite read exactly like one that never ran.

The claim was already in the source, and false. test_suite_lines is documented as skin-agnostic, and its docstring said "both skins render these same sentences, so a failed suite reads as failed everywhere". The chat view never called it. Anyone reading that function had been told the case was handled, which is why the gap survived D196 and this register.

The fix is the one ADR 011 already prescribes and D176 already used: render through the same function the complete report uses, so the skins cannot disagree. Four lines in a bounded view, which is what D160 bounded — volume, never attribution.

Closing test: test_every_test_suite_skin_names_the_resolved_program_not_just_command in tests/test_the_suite_names_the_program_it_ran.py, written by Codex against the rendered skins rather than the helper behind them.

The module-style resolution test in that file is not cited: D196 already made python3 -m pytest resolve its interpreter, so it passes at this entry's base and defends this change rather than proving it. It is marked as covering existing behaviour and kept, because a command whose program differs from its argv is the case a renderer is likeliest to get wrong.

Mutation: printing the resolved program in test_suite_lines alone — the literal instruction this cycle began with — passes every assertion about that function and leaves the chat view exactly as broken, because it does not call it; adding a pointer line instead of the sentences keeps the view bounded and still withholds what examined the value, which is the P8 violation rather than its symptom; correcting the docstring without rendering the section leaves a true sentence describing code that does not do it.

D200 — Closed: 330 findings asked for a voice this project does not write in, and a release chore nobody could remember (Low)

Two findings from reading this repository's own audit, closed together because both are about what a measurement is worth rather than what it says.

D401 was 330 of 566 pydocstyle findings. "First line should be in imperative mood" — every one asking for """Returns the score.""" to become """Return the score.""".

The docstrings here are deliberately not in that voice. They are narrative, and most exist to carry a defect's reasoning where the code that caused it lives: "Why each axis sits outside the score", "The delegate's own remediation prompt — its work order". Complying would flatten the sentences this project uses to explain itself, which trades the thing being measured for the measurement. It is also style rather than maintainability: a docstring's mood says nothing about whether code survives change.

Turned off in [tool.pydocstyle] with the reasoning beside it, rather than suppressed per finding — 330 acknowledgements with expiry dates would be a ritual, and the decision is about the whole repository's voice rather than any line. Every other rule stays on: missing docstrings, malformed summaries and bad quoting are still reported, which is the conformance half interrogate does not measure. Analyzer findings fell 832 to 502 and the documentation aspect did not move from 5.0, which is the evidence that nothing being measured was lost.

The version could not be reduced to one place in prose. D198 gave the number a single home in code. Four documents still have to state it, and each was guarded after going stale: the README's version line sat at 1.0.0 through five releases, and its Action example pinned v1.0.0 from 1.0.1 to 3.7.5 while action.yml runs pip install -e "$GITHUB_ACTION_PATH" — so that pin is what a reader installs.

Those guards are right and stay. What was wrong is that a person typed the number into each one: v3.7.9 was tagged and failed its release because the release plan's row was missed, and that row's check only fires once the tag exists, which is after anyone would have noticed. tools/stamp_version.py writes all four from the single source.

It does not improve change_coupling, and says so in its own docstring. Those files still move together because they must. This removes the chore, not the coupling — and the distinction matters, because the metric cannot tell enforced consistency from duplication, and this repository has both. The top six coupled pairs are the version, which is duplication; the next several are architecture.md with _architecture_layers.py at confidence 1.000, which is a gate working exactly as designed.

The first version of the tool rewrote this repository's history. Its SECURITY.md rule matched any N.N.x, including the sentence recording that an audit "found this table still naming 0.1.x eight release lines later" — the evidence for the entry explaining why that row is guarded at all. Caught by reading the diff, not by the tool. The rules are anchored to the table rows now and a test holds it.

Closing tests: test_the_tree_is_already_stamped and test_a_document_that_stops_matching_is_refused_not_skipped in tests/test_version_stamping.py.

tests/test_stamped_documents_stay_honest.py holds the two properties that pass at this entry's base — the documents are current, and the sentence recording why the table is guarded is intact. They defend the stamping tool rather than prove it, and are not cited.

Mutation: stamping the documents it can match and reporting success skips the one that drifted, which is exactly how v3.7.9 was tagged — so a rule that matches nothing exits non-zero and names the pattern; an unanchored SECURITY.md pattern passes every currency test and silently edits prose about past releases; suppressing D401 per finding rather than in configuration passes the same audit and leaves 330 expiring acknowledgements to renew.

D201 — Closed: a presentation the tool builds, validates and documents was offered by no question (Medium)

--format json has been accepted on both doors for eleven releases. The CLI parser lists it in --format's choices, docs/cli.md documents it, and the MCP tool validates format="json" and returns the report dictionary for it. Every surface that asks offered three options:

  • the terminal — "Report format — chat (Enter), markdown, or html: ",
  • the MCP default_format setup question, whose options were a hand-written ["chat", "markdown", "html"] rather than the tuple the terminal reads,
  • and therefore the skill and the server prompt, which derive their wording from that tuple and were correct about a set that was wrong.

So a person driving either door could only reach it by finding a flag in the CLI reference. That is the D150 shape — a question that exists and is never put — one option over rather than two questions over.

ADR 011 made it invisible to review as well. It decided "three user-facing skins" and never mentioned json in any of its 87 lines, so a reader checking whether the omission was deliberate found silence and could read it either way. It was read both ways in a single afternoon: called a defect, withdrawn as a recorded decision, then decided by the operator — "since it can already be built, may as well be a TTY option". The ADR now carries that amendment, and the reason given for excluding it (that json is the dictionary the others render rather than a rendering) is recorded as true and insufficient.

The fix is one tuple, not four edits. PRESENTATIONS gains json in fourth place; the MCP question's options and the MCP door's format validator now read it instead of restating it, which removes the two copies that let the offered set and the accepted set disagree. chat stays what Enter selects. At the terminal json behaves like chat and not like the file skins: it goes to stdout, because a person choosing it is about to pipe it.

Closing tests: test_every_format_the_cli_accepts_is_offered_at_the_question and test_choosing_json_at_the_terminal_renders_json in tests/test_every_built_presentation_is_offered.py. The population is the argument parser's own --format choices, read off the parser, so a sixth format accepted and not offered fails the same way.

tests/test_the_presentation_question_stays_answerable.py holds three properties that pass at this entry's base and are not cited: the prompt string names every option, every option the question offers is one a door accepts, and typing any of them returns it. They read the population from PRESENTATIONS, so at the base they held over three words and here over four — a check that adapts to the set cannot falsify a change to the set.

Mutation: adding json to the tuple and leaving the resolver alone passes the question tests and renders Markdown, because args.format or "markdown" answers a fourth choice with the second — the two file skins are immune only because they set a path; restating the options in _mcp_setup rather than importing them passes every test that reads PRESENTATIONS while the two doors offer different sets, which is the state this entry found; and asserting the three current words rather than deriving the population from argparse passes today and says nothing about the next format built.

D202 — Closed by decision: the hostile-audit brief is an unfinished feature, not a defect (Low)

--hostile-prompt-output and the maintainability-hostile-audit MCP prompt emit a 52-line brief seeded from a real run: the commit SHA, which analyzers ran, failed and were not applicable, the concepts nothing measured, a P1–P8 table with a falsification shape for each promise, and a five-point audit contract. It is deterministic and it is honest about being a seed — ADR 013 is explicit that the core seeds a hostile audit and never performs one. src/maintainability_audit/_hostile_prompt.py contains no network call, no model call and no import of either; build_report, _derive and the scorer never reference it; nothing in the audit path reaches it.

What is wrong is everything around it.

  1. It is in no document a user reads. README: no mention. Both SKILL.md copies: no mention. docs/product-intent.md discusses the adversarial audit practice twice and never records that this tool emits a brief for it. ADR 013 is the only place it exists, which is the same failure mode D201 records one file over.
  2. Its results reach nothing. Whatever an LLM concludes from the brief stays in that chat. No skin renders it, no field carries it, the history record does not know it happened. A deterministic tool whose most adversarial output is unattributable to any report is at least in tension with P8, even though the brief itself states its own provenance.
  3. It is an unfinished feature, not a defect. The seam was built and the half that closes the loop was not.

Decided 2026-09-21 by the operator: this is an unfinished feature. Recorded as a decision rather than left open, because an open entry naming a gap is an instruction to close it — and leaving it open is what caused the decision to be asked for twice and re-presented as a pending question a third time after it had already been given.

What follows from that: the three items above are not a defect queue. Nothing here is wrong in the arithmetic or in any rendered report, and the brief cannot influence a grade because no code path connects the emitter to the scorer. The work it implies — documenting the emitter, or carrying its results into the report — belongs to whatever the agentic audit feature turns out to be, and that is a product increment rather than a bug fix. No audit should re-derive this entry as an open defect from the P8 sentence that raised it.

Required behaviour: ADR 013 (the core seeds, never performs) and P8 (every reported value names what produced it). Neither is violated by the emitter; both are unserved by its absence from the record.

Mutation: whatever closes this, the check has to derive the surfaces a user reads from the shipped set rather than name them, because the instance in hand is three files (README, both SKILL.md copies) and the claim is about every document that describes what this tool emits — the exact shape thirty entries above this one got wrong. A check written from the instance passes while docs/product-intent.md, which discusses the practice twice and the emitter never, goes on saying nothing.

D203 — Closed: the written release process described the chore that had just been removed (Medium)

D198 reduced the version to one literal. D200 stamped the four documents that have to state it. Neither touched the instructions.

release.yml's header said "bump the version in pyproject.toml + __init__.py + config.py". CONTRIBUTING.md said "bump the version in all three places" and listed the same three, naming config.py VERSION — a constant D198 had deleted. Neither mentioned tools/stamp_version.py, which is the step that replaced the chore they were describing. CONTRIBUTING.md also told the releaser to cut the GitHub Release by hand, which a job has done automatically and idempotently since well before this.

Following the documented process reintroduces the exact copies D198 removed, and the suite then refuses the result. That is worse than a stale comment: the releaser arrives at a red build caused by the documentation, and the first thing anyone does with a test that fails on correct-looking work is suspect the test. Instructions that fight the gates are how a correct gate gets weakened.

Found by a Grok audit of 81caf1b, which also observed that the stamp tool "is not a release.yml step". Literally true and immaterial: the release build runs the whole suite against the installed wheel, and test_the_tree_is_already_stamped is in it. The enforcement was never missing. What was missing was any document that said so.

Closing tests: test_no_document_sends_a_releaser_to_a_file_the_version_left and test_every_release_instruction_names_the_stamping_step in tests/test_the_release_process_describes_itself.py.
