The population is every document that tells a releaser to annotate and push a version tag, found by searching the tree; the forbidden filenames are the copies D198 removed.

tests/test_the_release_record_stays_well_formed.py holds the positive half — that a release instruction names the one file a person types the version into — and is not cited, because it passes at this entry's base: both documents already named __init__.py. They named it alongside the two files the version had left, which is the defect, and saying where not to write it is only half an instruction.

Mutation: the population was first derived from the phrase "bump the version", which pulled in docs/migration-1.0.md — a sentence about a report's schema_version that has nothing to do with releasing — and the fix for that false positive is the shape of the real risk here: a check keyed to a phrase rather than to the act. Naming release.yml and CONTRIBUTING.md by hand passes today and says nothing about the third document, which is how this defect reached two files in the first place; and asserting only that the wrong filenames are absent passes on a document that names no file at all, which is why the one writer and the stamping step are asserted positively.

D204 — Closed: twelve consecutive releases published notes that said nothing (Medium)

The GitHub Release job extracts this version's ## X.Y.Z section from CHANGELOG.md and, finding none, writes Release X.Y.Z. and publishes that as the entire release notes.

CHANGELOG.md's last entry was 3.7.7. Tags 3.7.8 through 3.7.19 went out after it — twelve releases, each one publishing that single sentence to everybody watching the repository, including the three that closed Mediums in the security pillar and the one that changed what the MCP door accepts.

Nothing failed, and nothing could: an absent section and an intentionally terse one are the same thing to awk, and a substituted placeholder is indistinguishable from a deliberate one-line note from outside. The fallback is right for a re-pointed or recovery tag. It had quietly become the normal path.

The twelve sections are backfilled from the register and the release commits, and the check is placed in the ordinary suite rather than in the publishing job: by the time that job could notice, the tag exists, which is the same "after anyone would have noticed" that cost v2.11.0 a build (D124) and v3.7.9 its release (D200).

Closing tests: test_the_shipping_version_has_a_changelog_section and test_the_section_is_not_empty in tests/test_the_changelog_covers_the_shipping_version.py. The version is read from the package, so the check follows every bump without being edited.

tests/test_the_release_record_stays_well_formed.py also holds that no version is documented twice, and is not cited: it passes at this entry's base, where only seven versions were documented and none repeated. It is held because a duplicate heading publishes whichever came first and drops the other, and a backfill adding twelve sections under an existing heading is exactly how one gets written.

Mutation: asserting the section exists passes on a heading with nothing under it, which extracts to an empty notes.md and trips the same fallback — so the body is asserted separately; and asserting against a version named in the test rather than read from the package passes at the moment it is written and never again, which is the failure mode of every one of the twelve entries this backfills.

D205 — Closed: two version checks verified a proxy for what they claimed (Medium)

The gate's comment claimed something reading a file cannot do. It said "a tag is checked against the literal the wheel will actually carry". It reads src/maintainability_audit/__init__.py in the tree, before anything is built. That is a good gate — it fails in seconds rather than after an upload PyPI will not let anyone replace — but it is a check on the source, and the artifact is the thing that ships. The build step then installed the wheel, printed maintainability-agent --version, and compared it to nothing.

This is the sibling project's failure named in D198's own entry: secure-code-agent shipped a wheel stamped with one version while every report it produced carried another. test_the_dynamic_version_points_at_the_package holds the setuptools attribute; it does not hold the built metadata. Nothing did. The built wheel is now compared to the tag, and each comment says what its own check covers.

And the one-writer test searched for the current version's characters. test_only_one_module_writes_the_number swept every module for the string __version__ currently holds, which makes it blind in exactly the direction that matters: a module still carrying VERSION = "3.7.16" after the package moved to 3.7.20 is the drift the test exists to catch, and a substring search for "3.7.20" cannot see it. The check passed because the copy was stale. It now matches the shape of a version assignment — a module-level name containing "version" bound to a dotted number — so a second writer is caught whether or not it agrees with the first.

Both halves are one mechanism, which is why they close together: a check that verifies something adjacent to its claim, and reports on the adjacent thing.

Closing test: test_the_release_build_compares_the_artifact_to_the_tag in tests/test_the_version_has_one_source.py. It asserts against the workflow text, the way the existing gate check in that file already does, because the step runs in CI where no test does and what it is told to compare is the only thing checkable from here — and it asserts the comparison fails the build, because printing the number without comparing it is precisely what was there.

The strengthened sweep in that same file is deliberately not cited, and the reason is the finding itself. It cannot be proven by reverting the change, because the tree contains no stale copy to catch: a revert proof runs against a tree where the weak check and the strong check agree. The old check could only ever see a copy that matched the current version, so the only evidence it was weak is evidence it could not have produced. Mutation is the proof, below.

Mutation: planting RUBRIC_VERSION = "3.7.16" in _bands.py — a module neither version of the sweep names — passes the old search and fails the new one, and it is a stale literal rather than a fresh one precisely because a fresh copy was the only kind the old check could see. Separately, asserting that the build step merely prints the wheel's version passes on the code this entry found, which printed it and compared nothing.

D206 — Closed: a pillar's condition was a mean over an undisclosed subset of its aspects (Medium)

_condition averages the aspects that carried a number. That is the only honest arithmetic — a None is not a zero, and inventing one would be worse — and it means an unmeasured aspect is dropped rather than counted.

Maintainability declares seven aspects. Scored, with one bad one, they average 4.43. Withhold that one aspect and the same pillar reads 5.0, in a row that still lists all seven and says nothing. Both printed identically.

P3 is "withholding evidence cannot improve the reported grade", falsified by any input whose removal raises the reported field. P8 is "every report states what examined it", falsified by a reported value with no attributable source. This was both, in one cell.

The overall grade was already defended and the condition was not. _grade_on_the_floor prices unknowns at 0 and bands from the floor, so concealment is monotonically unprofitable there — which is why the audit's phrasing, "omitting analyzers can raise the grade", is right about the pillar reading and not about the graded field. Worth saying precisely, because the two have different fixes.

Flooring the condition was considered and rejected. ADR 007 defines it as the mean of measured aspects, and a delegated pillar's condition arrives already computed by the tool that owns it. Flooring one side would put two incomparable numbers in the same column, which is a worse lie than the one being fixed. Disclosure is the defence: every entry carries condition_coverage, and pillar_cells — already the one place both skins render a pillar row — names how many of how many aspects produced the number and which are missing.

"Could not measure" and "nothing to measure" are named separately. The scorer has always drawn that line for the grade, and the document kept only the consequence: a reader had to subtract the blocker text from the aspect layer to recover which was which, which is P8's second falsifier — a run whose coverage cannot be recovered from its output. score.not_applicable publishes it, additive and optional like analyzer_scored_dimensions before it, so no schema version moves.

One duplication fell out of it: SCORE_V2_FIELDS in tests/test_verified_grade.py was a second hand-typed copy of test_stage8_contract.CANONICAL, so one additive field had to be written into two lists that nothing compared. It is derived now — the difference between them is exactly the two fields stage 5 adds.

Closing tests: test_every_partial_condition_says_how_many_aspects_made_it, test_missing_evidence_and_a_resolved_absence_read_differently and test_withholding_the_worst_aspect_raises_the_number_and_says_so in tests/test_the_condition_says_what_it_measured.py. The population is PILLARS and each pillar's own declared aspects, read from the module that defines them, and the sweep withholds every aspect of every in-scope pillar one at a time rather than the one that motivated the entry.

tests/test_the_pillar_row_reads_cleanly.py holds that a complete condition carries no coverage note, and is not cited: it passes at this entry's base, where no row carried one. It is held because the obvious wrong version of this fix — annotate every row — satisfies every cited assertion above and teaches a reader to skip the sentence, which costs the disclosure the thing it was added for.

Mutation: the instance in hand is change_coupling under maintainability, and a check written from it passes while readability and testability say nothing — so the sweep is over every aspect of every in-scope pillar, and a sixth pillar is in the population the moment it is declared. Annotating every row rather than only partial ones passes the disclosure assertion and teaches a reader to skip the sentence, so the complete case is asserted silent. Printing one count for both kinds of absence passes every count assertion while an unknown reads as "nothing to measure", which is the collapse that makes silence read as fine.

D207 — Closed: the prompt forbade a change and then listed its target as work (Medium)

One prompt said two things. _withheld_paragraph prints "Not in scope for this change: N finding(s) need a design decision before code moves … Do not attempt them here." prompt_escalation_note prints that the escalated findings are deliberately excluded. Then prompt_focus_sections listed those same targets under "to inspect first", "to verify" and "to inspect".

withheld_reason is the one rule and it has two clauses — a Major Project, and a finding fixed before that came back. The focus lists honoured only the second, and only on three of their seven categories: hotspots, large files, risk findings. Duplicate blocks, near-duplicates, dead code and competing libraries were filtered by nothing at all, and no category honoured the Major Project clause.

This is D180's "one run authorising and forbidding the same change" on a different axis. It had already been fixed once — the audit of e88b429 found it in the risk-pattern list and the comment left behind says "the rule was enforced on every focus category except this one", which was true of the escalation clause and false of the whole rule. Written from the instance, not from the claim: the thirty-first instance of the register's own headline failure, in the file that fixed the thirtieth.

The fix is one rule, one locator, every category. withheld_targets reads every work-order item back through withheld_reason and emits one key each; _locate gained the competing-libraries shape, whose location is the minority package's example file, so both texts place a finding the same way.

Two keys, and they are not interchangeable. An identified finding is matched by identity, an unidentified one by location, and the fallback between them is wrong in both directions — which took two wrong attempts to establish. Falling back from identity to location let one class's withheld finding suppress another class's listed one: two huge overloads share two.py, and the fixture holding them also produces an unidentified duplication finding at one of their lines, so test_escalating_one_overload_does_not_hide_the_other failed for a reason it was not written for. Checking identity alone was wrong the other way only in a fixture that gave a risk-pattern item no fingerprint — a shape _items_from_counted never produces, because risk patterns are "the one class here the report gives a stable identity to". The fixture was corrected, not the rule.

Closing test: test_no_withheld_target_is_listed_as_work in tests/test_the_prompt_does_not_take_back_what_it_withheld.py. The population is every item the report's own work order marks withheld, read back through withheld_reason rather than listed here, and the report carries one finding of every class the focus sections render.

tests/test_the_prompt_keeps_listing_what_it_did_not_withhold.py holds three properties that pass at this entry's base and are not cited: that a run withholding nothing still lists everything, that the older escalation clause survives the rewrite, and that the fixture has not quietly lost a class. The first is the important one — the obvious wrong version of this fix is a filter that is really a mute button, and such a filter satisfies the cited test, which only ever asks whether withheld targets are absent.

Mutation: the claim-derived sweep caught two categories the first version of this fix missed — competing libraries, whose findings _locate could not place at all, and risk patterns, whose identity was being compared against an item keyed by location — so a check written from the duplicate-block instance would have shipped a fix that was still wrong for two of seven. Filtering everything rather than only the withheld trades a contradiction for an empty prompt, which is why a report that withholds nothing is asserted to list everything.

D208 — Closed: _work_order.py was five lines from its own file gate (Low)

src/maintainability_audit/_work_order.py is 745 lines against this repository's max_file_lines of 750, and past warn_file_lines of 600. D207 pushed it to 752 — a hard gate failure on this repository's own audit — and it was brought back under by shortening a docstring whose content is recorded in D207 instead.

That is a legitimate edit and it is not a fix. The next addition to this module fails the gate again, and the pressure to pass it will be pressure to delete a comment, which in this repository is the record of why a rule exists. Two have already been trimmed for length in one day (test_verified_grade, then here).

The module does two jobs, and the seam is visible: building work-order items from findings, and deciding who gets handed what — escalated_fingerprints, withheld_reason, withheld_targets, is_withheld, prompt_items, prompt_advised, prompt_targets. That is the same split _prompt_sections already makes from prompts, whose own docstring states the rule: "composing a document and building the pieces it composes are two jobs".

Filed Open rather than fixed inside D207, because it is a move of roughly two hundred and fifty lines across a module every prompt surface imports, and bundling a refactor of that size into the change that surfaced it would make both harder to review — the rule this tool's own remediation prompt states in every run.

Closed the same day, by the next change needing it. The fix after D207 required roughly ten lines in that module, which would have failed the gate again. The prediction in the paragraph above came true in one step, which is the argument for having written it down rather than trimming a second docstring.

_handoff now holds the whole question of who gets handed what: escalated_fingerprints, withheld_reason, withheld_targets, is_withheld, prompt_items, prompt_advised, prompt_targets and select. _work_order is 491 lines and decides what findings are worth and how they are ordered; the two grow for different reasons.

The boundary was already documented and enforced by nobody. architecture.md said presentation may import "_work_order's presentation-facing prompt selection" — a carve-out naming a module that did not exist. It exists now, so the rule is a module name rather than a sentence, and test_architecture holds it.

Required behaviour: this repository's own thresholds.max_file_lines, enforced by --fail-on-gate in CI, and the layering architecture.md states.

Closing tests: test_the_handoff_rules_are_defined_once, test_the_boundary_runs_one_way and test_neither_module_sits_on_its_own_file_gate in tests/test_the_handoff_rules_are_their_own_module.py.

A pure move changes no behaviour, so the falsifier is the boundary. The first draft of this entry cited nothing and said so, which prove_falsifiers refused — correctly: a closed entry that defends nothing can be undone by the next person who finds the import inconvenient. What is falsifiable is that the seam exists and runs one way. The population is _handoff's own public surface, read from the module, so a rule added there later is covered without editing the test; the file-length assertion reads this repository's published thresholds rather than repeating the number that failed at 752.

Mutation: moving lines out until the number drops below 750 leaves the same module doing two jobs and the same pressure one addition away, so the cut is the seam rather than a line count — _work_order imports nothing from _handoff, which is what makes it a boundary rather than a shuffle. Leaving the moved names re-exported from _work_order would have spared every caller an edit and restored the cycle the split exists to prevent.

D209 — Closed: a clone was filed at one of its copies and told to be removed (Medium)

A duplicate block is a statement about two or more locations. The work order carries one path — _locate takes the first, which is the right place to send someone — and the item read "duplicated block in billing.py" with the target "remove the duplicated block".

An agent handed that deletes billing.py's copy. That clears nothing, because the duplication is a relationship, and it breaks the file the copy was deleted from. The half of the finding that makes extract the right verb is the other location, and no surface carried it.

"Remove" is the word D165 already had to correct, one class over: for risk patterns it read as "delete the matched text", which cleared the finding and left the debt. Same verb, same failure, and the fix there — say what resolving it means instead of naming the finding — is the fix here.

Near-duplicates had the same gap from the other side. Their item said "remove the near-duplicate declaration" and never named what it nearly duplicates, while near_duplicate_section in the same prompt already rendered "near.py:5 copy is 95% identical to orig at orig.py:1". One renderer had it and the work order did not, which is D157's shape: a claim fixed on one surface of one report.

And the first version of this fix reproduced that shape. It put the other locations in the bounded prompt and left the copy-paste block — the one that travels alone into an agent with no report around it — still naming one place. other_sites is now shared by both, because a rule about what a prompt says that lives in two places is how one of them gets fixed alone.

On the shipped demo tree the block now reads Location: billing.py:16 / Also at: invoices.py:15, where it previously named one file and asked for a deletion.

Closing tests: test_a_finding_about_several_places_carries_all_of_them, test_no_multi_place_item_is_told_to_remove_one_copy and test_every_surface_that_prints_a_location_prints_the_others in tests/test_a_clone_names_every_copy.py. The population is COUNTED_SOURCES — every finding class the report carries as a located list, read from the module that defines them — and which of those are about more than one place is answered by _sites, the same function the builder uses, rather than by a list in the test.

Mutation: asserting the item dict carries the locations passes while every rendered surface still prints one, which is exactly what the first attempt did; so the two surfaces that print Location: are rendered and searched. Fixing the location and leaving the verb passes a check about locations while still instructing a deletion, so the target is asserted separately. Attaching a site list to dead code or a risk pattern — one declaration, one match — puts a second location in a prompt that has one, so the single-place classes are asserted to gain nothing.

D210 — Closed: an unmeasured pillar reported this repository's practice as its own (Medium)

Practice level is a fact about the repository — is a linter wired into CI, is there a coverage gate, are ADRs maintained. The pillar section states it once, above the table: "Practice level 4 of 5". Every row then repeated that one number in its own Practice column, including the rows for pillars this tool does not measure.

So the efficiency row read practice 4 for a pillar ADR 007 declares out of scope, and an unmeasured security row said the same. A fact about the repository became a claim about a pillar nobody took a reading on, sitting beside a dash in the Condition column that said so.

posture was nulled for exactly this reason and practice was left behind. _measured_entry's own comment records the first version printing "efficiency — healthy" from the practice axis alone: "a maturity level vouching for code the tool had explicitly declared out of scope, which is the exact silence-reads-as-fine defect this pillar block exists to end." The fix nulled the derived cell and left its input, so the number kept printing.

Both axes are now absent for a pillar with no reading of its own, and one shared practice_cell prints the dash, beside pillar_cells which already does the same for condition.

A delegated pillar that came back with a document keeps its number, and it is a different one: secure-code-agent measures security practice and reports its own level, which is a real measurement of that pillar rather than this repository's standing in for one.

Closing tests: test_an_unmeasured_pillar_carries_no_practice_level, test_no_skin_prints_a_digit_for_an_unmeasured_pillar and test_a_delegated_pillar_with_a_document_reports_the_producers_level in tests/test_an_unmeasured_pillar_reports_no_number.py. The population is PILLARS, read from the module that declares them.

Mutation: the audit reported this on the security row; efficiency had the identical row for the identical reason, so a check written from the reported instance ships a fix that is still wrong one row up — the population is every pillar outside this tool's own measurement. Nulling the field and leaving the renderer on str(None) passes an assertion about the field and puts the word "None" in the table, so the rendered cell is asserted separately. Blanking the column everywhere passes both and removes the axis ADR 007 exists to keep beside condition, so an in-scope pillar is asserted to still report it, and the delegated fixture's level differs from this repository's so the two cannot be confused.

D211 — Closed: the audited tree could redirect a read out of itself (Medium)

config.repository_path has always bounded a configured path: it resolves the target, refuses one outside the root, then walks the lexical route and refuses a symlinked component — because an inward .maintainability -> src link passes a containment check on the resolved path and shows only on the route (D34). Its refusal states the principle outright: "the audited tree cannot redirect where this agent reads or writes."

Three readers take their paths from the audited tree and never went through it: practice detection, test-command detection and the generated-file banner. D147 routed them through the regular-file door, which checks what a path is. Nothing checked where it goes.

The consequence was measurable, not theoretical. A repository whose pyproject.toml is a symlink to a file outside its root scores practice level 2 with a linter-config signal; the same tree with an ordinary in-root file scores 1. The audited tree raised its own reported level using a file this tool was never pointed at — and practice level feeds the pillar posture, so it reaches the report a reader acts on.

That is P3's shape reflected. P3 forbids withholding evidence from improving a reported value; this was planting evidence outside the boundary to improve one.

refuse_symlinked_route moved from config down into _operator_reads, which is where the reading primitive lives and which config already reads through — so the rule sits below its callers rather than above them. open_repository_file composes it with the containment check and the regular-file door, and both checks are kept because either alone leaks: the route catches an inward link that resolves back inside, the containment check catches an absolute target the walk never meets.

The operator door stays exempt, and says so. An operator names their own config and a symlinked config is an ordinary setup; the difference this entry turns on is who chose the path.

_banner is the one reader left on the unbounded door, stated rather than overlooked: it is called only from the discovery walk, on a path that walk produced, and discovery does not descend an outbound symlink — a tree carrying leaked.py -> ../outside.py audits with files_scanned: 1, verified before this entry was written. Its input is bounded before it is handed over, and the test names it as the sole exemption so a fourth reader cannot join it quietly.

Closing tests: test_an_outbound_symlink_is_refused, test_an_inward_symlink_is_refused_too and test_the_tree_cannot_raise_its_own_practice_level in tests/test_the_tree_cannot_redirect_a_read.py. The population is derived from the doors the modules actually call, so a fourth reader appears in a failure message rather than in nobody's list.

Mutation: checking containment alone passes the outbound case and lets an inward shadow -> real link through, which is the half D34 exists for; walking the route alone passes that and lets an absolute target the walk never meets through. Asserting a fixed practice level rather than equality with the unlinked tree goes stale the first time the rubric moves, so the worked example compares the two trees. And a boundary that refused everything would pass every refusal assertion while measuring nothing, so an ordinary in-tree file is asserted to still read.

Related: tests/test_operator_named_reads.py's single-resolution check was scoped from the whole module to the functions that perform the read. Scanning the file was a proxy that held while the file held only the read path; a route walk resolves ancestor names because that is what a route walk is. It runs before any handle exists, inspects ancestors rather than the target, and the file is still opened once and validated through that one handle — so the property is unchanged and the proxy was replaced with the claim.

D212 — Closed: C#'s primary loop was not a branch (Medium)

foreach is how C# iterates. The C-family complexity pattern matched \bfor\b, and that word boundary falls after for — foreach has an e there, so it never matched. Two methods that do the same work:

WithForeach   complexity=1  cognitive=0
WithFor       complexity=2  cognitive=2

The first contains a loop. Both metrics read it as straight-line code, and every rate computed from them is a rate over a denominator that missed it.

This is the fourth instance of one class, and the first three were each fixed alone. Fortran's do: "six nested loops once scored complexity 1 because the pattern did not know the keyword." Swift's guard and repeat: "a function built from guards reads as having no branches at all, which is the Fortran do defect in another language." PHP's elseif and foreach, added after checking every construct against a reference implementation: "a dispatch chain scored zero."

Each note names the previous one. None of them produced a check over every language, so the fifth instance was already sitting in the shared C-family pattern that PHP's foreach note is printed beside — the same keyword, missing in two places, fixed in one.

The independent-implementation check disagreed, and the grammar settles it. test_every_construct_agrees_with_an_independent_implementation reported ForEach: ours=2 lizard=1, and its own message says neither number is authoritative. The C# specification defines foreach (V v in x) by its expansion: a while (e.MoveNext()) loop over an enumerator. The loop condition is in the language's own definition of the statement, and while is counted here and by lizard alike. Microsoft's metric and SonarQube both count it. Declared in DECLARED_DIVERGENCES with that reasoning, which is what that mechanism is for — and a reminder the file already records in another entry: lizard is a second opinion, not an oracle.

Closing tests: test_an_idiomatic_loop_costs_what_a_counted_loop_costs and test_a_loop_is_never_free in tests/test_every_language_counts_its_own_loop.py, parametrised over ten languages.

tests/test_every_language_has_a_loop_fixture.py holds the population rule — every parsed language owes a pair — and is not cited, because the fixtures it counts live in the test file and so it passes at this entry's base. It is the mechanism whose absence let one defect survive three fixes: nothing ever asked whether the other nine languages had the same hole.

Mutation: asserting the two spellings agree is satisfied by a pattern that knows neither, which is the Fortran do defect passing its own test with both sides at 1 — so a loop is separately asserted never to cost what a branchless routine costs. Writing the check for C# alone repeats exactly what the three previous fixes did, so it is parametrised over every parsed language and a language with no fixture fails rather than being skipped. Taking lizard as the oracle would have reverted the fix instead of declaring the divergence.

D213 — Closed: a question published one choice and accepted any number (Medium)

The three labor rates published options: [90], [140], [210] — a one-item enumeration — and meant it as a type marker. The elicitation model builder reads a non-string option list as "float field, any value", so that door worked: any rate went through.

A host on the data path reads options as what the word says and offers the single value. Submitting a real senior rate meant contradicting the published contract, which is what the operator hit entering 130/165/275 against a published 90/140/210 — he got the rates in, by ignoring what the tool said it would accept.

One field meant two things depending on which door read it. That is the D189/D190/D193 family, where the elicitation path and the data path disagreed about the same setup; this instance had them disagreeing about the type of an answer rather than about which questions to ask. It is also D191's shape reversed: there a completed setup produced a file the tool's own contract rejected, here the contract offered less than the code accepted.

type: "number" says what the field is, and default goes back to being a suggestion rather than the only permitted answer. Both readers ask the question what it is instead of inferring it from the shape of its options.

Closing test: test_no_question_offers_a_single_choice in tests/test_a_published_question_offers_what_it_accepts.py. The population is every published question across both stages, so a fourth question given a one-item enumeration as a type hint is caught the same way rather than only the three rates.

Only the published shape falsifies, and the reason is the finding. tests/test_a_setup_question_keeps_its_contract.py holds the rest and is not cited: submitting 130 against a published options: [90] worked before this change, because the validator already accepted any number while the question offered one. That asymmetry was the defect, so a test of the accepting half cannot fail at this entry's base. It is kept because the rates must still arrive as floats — a rate that never converted is what left the economic context pending forever in D193 — and because the refusals guard the branch this change touched on its way past.

Mutation: dropping the enumeration could have dropped the validation with it, which accepts "ninety" and carries a string into money — so a non-numeric rate is asserted to still be refused, and the coerced value is asserted to be a float because a rate that never converted is exactly what left the economic context pending forever in D193. Touching the numeric branch also touches the one that refuses depth: "nuclear", so a real menu is asserted to still refuse what it does not offer. And checking the three rates rather than sweeping every question would leave the next one-item enumeration to be found by another operator.

Disposition

Every entry is closed. The four from the post-2.11.0 audit closed on 2026-09-08: They are the remainder of the audit of origin/main after 2.11.0 (1704498) through #198, with the 3.0.0 publish claims left for when that release is actually out. D138 is the D133 class still open: a hunk-only diff, the paste an agent actually sends, still reads as clean on every brace language. D139 is the D129 sentence that survived the honest paragraph. D140 is the scoring claim #198 shipped: two counting conventions published as one analyzer rate, which is what 3.0.0 will re-grade on. D141 is the D131 remainder: scan reads were carved out of the primitive without being named as a limit.

D137 closed on 2026-09-07: the declarations criterion-set check was written at repository scope and applied to units, so one Python file let a C repository be scored on two of three criteria. It is now checked per language. That fix changes which repositories use analyzer readings, so it moves the corpus declarations reference and rides the recalibration release rather than shipping alone. D136 is closed by decision rather than by code — --check stays on the CLI for now, recorded as a non-goal so it is not re-derived as an unfinished bug. They are the remainder of the audit of the twenty commits on main after 2.8.0. D125–D129 (the 2.11.0 language findings from that same audit) closed in 2.11.1. D130 closed with it: --sarif-input now reads through read_operator_file, and every CLI option is classified by what it does with a path so a fourth one cannot be added unclassified — the count in prose that D104 relied on is no longer the control.

D131 closed after it: all eight sites across five modules read through the primitive, and the guard now parses every state-file module rather than the two --sarif-input needed. It was briefly marked closed on the strength of the --sarif-input fix alone and reopened — the same mistake D104 made, closing a class from one instance, caught this time before it shipped. D132 closed: cognitive complexity is a named budget now, and a fail no budget names reports no figure rather than a negative one. D133 closed — a piped diff is now refused by its format in every language, where the sentence promising it had been true of Python alone. D134 closed: the two doors share one refusal list now, so the flag-refusal class --staged named cannot drift out of --check again. D135 closed: headroom reports every budget a declaration can fail on, and bands on the worst of them. D136 is the chat-primary gap: --check is CLI-only, and it was decided on 2026-09-06 that it stays there for now. D124 is a release-checklist omission that cost a build and no artifact: the gate held, the tag was re-pointed. D123 is the shipped README contradicting its own table, found by reading the page rather than by any check. D121 and D122 were found by CI on the pull request that shipped the rest, after a green local suite — an empty parameter set reported as a pass, and f-strings unmasked on every Python from 3.12. Both are the same shape as the audit that found them: a check that could not fail, and a fix that reached one interpreter. D112 through D120 record a complexity audit that found this project measuring Python against its own comments, missing its boolean operators, and reading a ? in type position as a ternary in five languages — nine entries, of which D119 is the method failure behind the other eight, and D120 was found by the check D119 built. D110 closed once 2.9.0 put the images on main and both absolute URLs returned 200. D109 closed the hollow test and the gate hole that let it through, and D111 closed the escape phrase that the gate matched inside prose about itself. D108's closing test has landed. D106 preserves both determinism checks with explicit double invocations and failure messages. SonarCloud confirmation awaits CI. D107 records two SonarCloud false-positive resolutions in the same turn they were taken.

Everything before them is closed. D102 closed by splitting the two helpers that were over the cognitive warn line; D101 and D103 closed the day they were filed — D103 by splitting _jobs, D101 by the decision that an unsupported platform is a stated limit and never a reason to weaken the supported ones. All three were filed 2026-09-05, once it was noticed that the rule being broken was the known-defect rule. A Windows probe had produced a High finding about the write-safety path, and two warn-level findings had been resolved in PR threads without being acted on — and all three were reported in a wrap-up as "still open" rather than filed, which is the exact inversion of the known-defect rule. 2.8.0 was then tagged and released with them open, against "no release until the known-defect ledger is empty". The release is not withdrawn; the entries are filed and the ledger gates the next one.

Until 2026-09-05 the ledger was empty. D100 was the last to close, on 2026-08-28: the artifact had promoted itself to a 1.0 release candidate with no recorded authorization, and the entry that did it was the entry written to stop exactly that. It is the fifth round running in which the defect was in a fix rather than in the code the fix repaired, and the first in which the register could not say who wrote the fix.

D89 closed the same day, when the resolve-constraints job produced the Linux closure the gating jobs had been pinned against a macOS resolution of. Its xfail(strict=True) residual became XPASS(strict) on the day the file landed, which is the behaviour that marker exists for: it fails when the situation is fixed, so the fix cannot be taken while the marker is left behind.

This page reached an empty ledger twice and gave it back twice, which is the useful part. An audit observed that the register can be empty while the proof it cites is failing in CI, and that "0 open" was being read as "known good". A closure that does not survive being checked is not a closure.

Where the rest came from. D32 through D46 came from two independent security audits on 2026-08-23 and closed over the two days after; D47 through D49 came from the chat-surface work that preceded them; D50 through D55 came from UAT preparation on 2026-08-25 — one from reading the question set and five from an audit of the whole repository; D56 through D62 came from an audit of the whole repository on 2026-08-26, which also reopened D38. Its verdict was that the register was "an empty ledger, not an empty defect list", and on every finding it was right. D63 came from asking who the product is for, on 2026-08-26. D64 through D70 came from a second round of both audits on 2026-08-26, run against the fixes for the first — four of the seven reopened an entry that had already been closed once, and three of those reopens went through the closing test rather than around the fix.

D70 is the one worth reading twice. The local baseline this project diffs every change against — 101 failures and 191 errors, carried run after run as "known" — was a single line in seven test fixtures pinning PATH=/usr/bin:/bin, which on this project's own development machine resolves to Xcode's stub rather than to git. None of those 292 results said anything about the product, and any real regression landing in those files had 292 places to hide. The discipline of diffing against a baseline was working exactly as designed and was protecting a number that meant nothing. The count is derived from the headings above and checked by test_the_disposition_names_the_entries_that_are_open, which required this sentence rather than an omission — with nothing open, a disposition that simply lists no entries reads exactly like a parser that stopped working. D34 through D37 were filed by the same 2026-08-23 security pass and have since closed; this paragraph named them as open for two days after they were not, which is the register describing a state a reader cannot verify from its own headings. The count is now derived from the headings above rather than carried in prose, and test_the_disposition_names_the_entries_that_are_open fails if the two disagree.

Those four are the reason v1.0 does not cut. Two are the classes this register already paid to close and never applied everywhere: D18 bound the skill installer's writes by descriptor, and config, history and baseline still open by name; D20 bounded paths.history, and class_dirs and expand_files still take unbounded paths from the audited tree. One, D35, is a trust inversion — product-intent.md P1 says a user enables tool acquisition, and a repository config overrides the user tier, so a pull request can make the host run npx --yes.

The lesson is not that the fixes were wrong. It is that each was applied to the seam an audit happened to be looking at. "Close the class" means every door that shares the primitive, and three rounds of chat-surface auditing never looked at the write primitives at all.

D28 and D29 were opened and closed on 2026-08-22 under the standing rule that a release ships only from an empty known-defect ledger; filing them here rather than leaving them in a chat message is what made them countable. D30 and D31 came from the audit of D21–D27 and are the most instructive pair in the register: both are defects in the fixes, found because the fixes were audited rather than trusted. D30 is a precondition that guarded one door of four. D31 is a check that verified a falsifier existed without verifying a reader could find it. D32 is the pair's own audit round: a refusal that read correctly in-process and arrived at the client as an internal error, a CLI that crashed where the chat doors refused politely, and a title that claimed more than its fix delivered. D33 is the round after that, and the pattern held a fourth time: a check that verified a citation named a test, satisfied by the filename in the path; a refusal translated on one layer of two; two parsers reading one file to opposite conclusions.

Four rounds running, every defect has been in a fix rather than in the code it repaired. That is not a run of bad luck. A fix is written by someone who has just convinced themselves they understand the problem, and it is tested by someone in that same state — so the test tends to assert the thing the author was already thinking about. Auditing a fix as hard as the thing it repairs is the only step that has reliably caught this, and every round of it has paid.

Every closed entry sits behind a test that would fail if its defect returned, and since 2026-08-22 that citation is machine-checked — three entries had been closing on tests a rename had deleted. The count is read from the entries themselves, never asserted as a number that stops describing the register when it grows. D15 was reopened twice: once when an audit found its close had rewritten the requirement, once when the proof turned out to be vacuous. D18 and D19 were each reopened after their first close, when audits reproduced a descriptor race, a hard-link write-through, a short write, and three kinds of occupancy the installer ignored. D20 was found by audit in the MCP write boundary and is the one security defect in this register: a repository could name a history path outside itself and be believed. D21 and D22 came from the same field run and are the same shape: an instruction surface that left a gap, and an agent that filled it by improvising a question the tool already asks. D23 is the most consequential entry here — nine releases shipped with no analyzer catalog inside them, so every installed copy lost the analyzer pool while a green suite, running from a checkout, reported everything working. D24 is how that outage stayed quiet: the envelope reported the request rather than the result. D25 corrected D22's diagnosis rather than extending it — the presentation step was a real gap, but the reason a user was never once offered the html report is that the question offering it was handed back as data no instruction surface mentioned. Three entries in this register are the same sentence: the product produced the right thing and nothing told the agent to use it. D26 is the one that stopped treating that as a documentation problem — the audit no longer runs at all until the repository has been set up, so there is no premature grade for an agent to report in place of the questions. D47 is the entry that had to be corrected after it was written: it was opened as the cause of a field report and was not the cause. D48 is the one found by a machine rather than a person — an unchanged main went red when a dependency shipped, exposing a misclassification the product had been shipping since its first MCP release — and the anticipated set was wrong twice in opposite directions before a derivation test owned it. D49 is D23's hole on the skill payload; it is not the cause of the stale installed skill that prompted the look.
